The h ∗ h^{*} -polynomials of type C hypersimplices
Antoine Abram, Jose Bastidas

Abstract
. We study the Ehrhart theory of hypersimplices of type C, as introduced by Lam and Postnikov for general crystallographic root systems. The h ∗ h^{*} -polynomials of classical hypersimplices are known to relate to various Eulerian statistics on the symmetric group. In this paper, we introduce a new statistic and partial order on signed permutations, which we use to derive explicit formulas for the h ∗ h^{*} -polynomials of type C hypersimplices. Additionally, we explore connections with other statistics, including flag-excedances and circular descents, flag-descents, and Coxeter descents.



Keywords:

Alcoved polytopes, Signed permutation, permutation statistics, Coxeter groups, Weak order, Ehrhart
h^{*}
-polynomials.

1.
Introduction

Let
P\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
d
-dimensional polytope with vertices on a lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Ehrhart
[
12
]
showed that the function

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}L_{P}}=L^{\Lambda}_{P}:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}:r\mapsto|rP\cap\Lambda|

is a polynomial of degree
d
in
r
.
Later, Stanley
[
33
]
showed that the polynomial
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}h_{P}^{*}(t)}
, defined from
L_{P}(r)
by

(1)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-h*}\sum_{r\geq 0}L_{P}(r)t^{r}=\frac{h_{P}^{*}(t)}{(1-t)^{d+1}},

has
only
nonnegative integer coefficients.
The polynomial
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}h_{P}^{*}(t)}
is known as the
(Ehrhart)
h^{*}
-polynomial
of
P
.
We refer the reader to
[
5
, Chapter 3]
for a comprehensive introduction to Ehrhart theory.

Understanding the coefficients of
L_{P}
and
h^{*}_{P}
for arbitrary polytopes
P
is a difficult problem that has motivated substantial research in recent years; see, for instance,
[
8
,
9
,
13
,
14
,
18
,
19
,
22
,
32
]
.
However the sum of the coefficients of
h^{*}_{P}
is simple to understand: it equals
\Vol(P)
, the normalized volume of
P
.

In this paper, we study the
h^{*}
-polynomial of
type C hypersimplices
.

The classical (type A) case

For positive integers
k<n
, the (type A)
hypersimplex

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Delta_{n,k}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the polytope whose vertices are all the
0/1
-vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with exactly
k
ones.
Hypersimplices are fundamental objects in algebraic combinatorics; for example, they are the matroid polytopes of uniform matroids and also arise as the image of the Grassmannian under the moment map.

A classical result, implicit in the work of Laplace, states that the volume of the hypersimplex is given by the
Eulerian numbers
. Specifically,

(2)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:A-vol-Eul}\Vol(\Delta_{n,k})={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A_{n-1,k-1}}:=|\{w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}\mid\des(w)=k-1\}|,

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\des(w)}
denotes the
descent statistic
(the number of positions
i
such that
w_{i}>w_{i+1}
).
Thus, the coefficients of the
h^{*}
-polynomial of the hypersimplices
refine
the Eulerian numbers.
Two decades ago, Katzman
[
23
]
provided an explicit formula for these coefficients, though it was not manifestly positive. Only recently, Kim
[
24
]
proved a conjecture by Early
[
10
]
, offering a combinatorial interpretation of these coefficients using a particular type of decorated set partitions. However, we are not aware of an interpretation of these coefficients via a joint distribution of two statistics on
\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
.

For the
half-open hypersimplices

\Delta^{\prime}_{n,k}
, obtained from
\Delta_{n,k}
by removing the facet lying in the hyperplane
x_{n}=1
when
k\geq 2
, Li
[
28
]
provided the following two elegant, explicitly positive formulas for their
h^{*}
-polynomials using permutation statistics:

(3)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Li^{\prime}s}h_{\Delta^{\prime}_{n,k}}^{*}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}\\
\des(w)=k-1\end{subarray}}t^{{\rm cover}(w)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad h_{\Delta^{\prime}_{n,k}}^{*}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}\\
\exc(w)=k-1\end{subarray}}t^{\des(w)}.

Here
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\exc}
denotes the
excedance
statistic and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rm cover}(w)}
denotes the number of elements covered by
w
in a certain partial order defined on
\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
. This partial order is–up to a simple relabeling–exactly the order on cyclic permutations defined by Abram, Chapelier-Laget, and Reutenauer
[
1
]
. The second formula was originally conjectured by Stanley. Observe that, since
\des
and
\exc
have the same distribution, plugging
t=1
in either of the formulas above recovers
Equation

2
.

New results for type C hypersimplices

In a series of influential papers, Lam and Postnikov
[
25
,
26
]
define generalized hypersimplices for any crystallographic root system
\Phi
.
The
\Phi
-hypersimplex
\Delta_{\Phi,k}
is determined by inequalities of the form
0\leq\langle x,\alpha\rangle\leq 1
for all simple roots
\alpha
and
k-1\leq\langle x,\theta\rangle\leq k
, where
\theta
is the highest root of
\Phi
.

Explicitly, for the
root system of type
C_{n}
with simple roots
2e_{1}\,,\,e_{2}-e_{1}\,,\,e_{3}-e_{2},\dots,e_{n}-e_{n-1}
, these polytopes are

(4)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-hypersimplex}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Delta_{C_{n},k}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid 0\leq 2x_{1}\,,\,x_{2}-x_{1}\,,\,\dots\,,\,x_{n}-x_{n-1}\leq 1\,\text{ and }\,k-1\leq 2x_{n}\leq k\},

for
k=1,\dots,2n-1
.
The
half-open hypersimplex
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}
is obtained by replacing the weak inequality
k-1\leq 2x_{n}
in
Equation

4
by the strict inequality
k-1<2x_{n}
; except at
k=1
, for which
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},1}:=\Delta_{C_{n},1}
.
The vertices of
\Delta_{C_{n},k}
lie on the half-integer lattice
{\Lambda:=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}}
, with respect to which we compute its Ehrhart and
h^{*}
-polynomial.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathfrak{B}_{n}}
denote the group of
signed permutations
: permutations
w
of the set
\{-n,\dots,-1,1,\dots,n\}
such that
w(-i)=-w(i)
. Our formulas are given in terms of statistics on the following subset of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Definition 1
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}X_{n}}\subset\mathfrak{B}_{n}
be the following collection of signed permutations:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}X_{n}}:=\{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\mid w^{-1}(1)>0\}.

Our first result relies on a shelling of the unimodular triangulation of
\Delta_{C_{n},k}
as an
alcoved polytope
. Explicitly, we construct a partial order on
X_{n}
whose cover relations are determined by the
big ascent
statistic
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\basc}
introduced in
Section

5
. This poset serves as a combinatorial model for a certain interval of the
weak order
on the alcoves of the affine reflection arrangement of type C.

Theorem A
.

For
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
, the
h^{*}
-polynomial of the half-open hypersimplex
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}
is

h_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}^{*}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in X_{n}:\\
\cdes(w^{-1})=k\end{subarray}}t^{\basc(w)}.

The statistic
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\cdes}
above is the
circular descent statistic
of Lam and Postnikov
[
26
]
, which we review in
Section

2.2.3
. By plugging
t=1
in
Theorem

A
, we essentially recover their volume formula for generalized hypersimplices in
[
26
, Theorem 9.3]
; see
Equation

17
.

The half-open hypersimplices partition the
fundamental parallelepiped

(5)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-PiC}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Pi_{C_{n}}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid 0\leq 2x_{1}\,,\,x_{2}-x_{1}\,,\,\dots\,,\,x_{n}-x_{n-1}\leq 1\}.

Given that the fundamental parallelepiped and all the half-open hypersimplices have the same dimension,
Theorem

A
implies the following formula for the
h^{*}
-polynomial of
\Pi_{C_{n}}
:

(6)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:h-parallel1}h_{\Pi_{C_{n}}}^{*}(t)=\sum_{k}h_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}^{*}(t)=\sum_{w\in X_{n}}t^{\basc(w)}.

The parallelepiped
(\Pi_{C_{n}},\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n})
is
integrally equivalent
to the box
([0,1]\times[-1,1]^{n-1},\mathbb{Z}^{n})
.
By studying the triangulation obtained by slicing this box with the linear Coxeter arrangement of type
BC_{n}
, one finds an alternative formula for the
h^{*}
-polynomial of
\Pi_{C_{n}}
:

(7)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:h-parallel2}h_{\Pi_{C_{n}}}^{*}(t)=\sum_{w\in X_{n}}t^{\des_{B}(w)},

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\des_{B}}
denotes the usual descent statistic of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
as a Coxeter group.

A natural question arises by comparing
Equations

6
and
7
:

Is there a formula for the
h^{*}
-polynomial of
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}

that uses the descent statistic of the hyperoctahedral group?

The following result gives a positive answer to this question.

Theorem B
.

For all
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
, the
h^{*}
-polynomial of the half-open hypersimplex
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}
is

h_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}^{*}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in X_{n}:\\
\fexc(w)=k-1\end{subarray}}t^{\des_{B}(w)},

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\fexc}
denotes the
flag-excedance
statistic
of Foata and Han
[
16
]
.

We prove this theorem in
Section

4
, by manipulation of generating functions. The definition of
\fexc
and of the other statistics on signed permutations that appear in the formulas above are reviewed in
Section

2.1
.
Evaluating at
t=1
, we find a new formula for the volume of the type C hypersimplices that is fundamentally different from that of Lam and Postnikov.

Corollary C
.

For all
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
,

\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},k})=|\{w\in X_{n}\mid\fexc(w)=k-1\}|.

The statistic
\exc_{B}
defined by
\exc_{B}(w)=\lfloor\tfrac{\fexc(w)+1}{2}\rfloor
(see
[
3
,
4
]
) is Eulerian, meaning its distribution on
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is the same as that of
\des_{B}
. Using
Corollary

C
, we can recover the
type B Eulerian numbers
B_{n,k}
in terms of volumes of type C hypersimplices; c.f.
Equation

2
.

Corollary D
.

For all
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
, we have:

\displaystyle B_{n,k}

\displaystyle=\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k-1})+2\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k})+\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k+1})

where
\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},j})=0
whenever
j<1
or
j>2n-1
.

In type A, the fundamental parallelepiped
\Pi_{A_{n+1}}
is equivalent to the unit cube
[0,1]^{n}
, whose Ehrhart polynomial is
(r+1)^{n}
. Thus, Worpitzky’s identity tells us that the
h^{*}
-polynomial of
\Pi_{A_{n+1}}
is the Eulerian polynomial
E_{A_{n}}(t)=\sum_{k}A_{n,k}t^{k}
.
In
Section

7
, we obtain the corresponding result in type C by explicitly computing the Ehrhart series of
\Pi_{C_{n+1}}
.

Theorem E
.

The following identity holds

\sum_{n\geq 0}h^{*}_{\Pi_{C_{n+1}}}(t)\frac{x^{n}}{n!}=e^{3x(t-1)}{\rm Eul}_{A}(t,2x)^{2},

where
{\rm Eul}_{A}(t,x)
is the exponential generating function of the Eulerian polynomials.

As a byproduct of the proof of the formula above, we also deduce the real-rootedness of the
h^{*}
-polynomial of
\Pi_{C_{n}}
; see
Theorem

32
.

In light of
Equation

6
,
Theorem

E
can be interpreted as providing the generating function for the big ascent statistic on the sequence of posets
\{(X_{n},\leq)\}_{n\geq 1}
. In
Section

9
, we show that in a precise sense, the
limit
of
X_{n}
is the lattice
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{SP}}
of
strict integer partitions
. This provides a type C analog of a result in
[
1
]
, where the authors show that the posets of cyclic permutations
converge
to Young’s lattice of integer partitions.

Related work

Li’s work on the
h^{*}
-polynomial of the (type A) half-open hypersimplices
[
28
]
sparked significant interest in these polytopes. In
[
19
]
, Hibi, Li, and Ohsugi computed the face vectors of these polytopes. Han and Josuat-Vergès
[
18
]
computed
h^{*}
-polynomials for slices of dilated hypercubes using flag statistics on colored permutations; their approach does not rely on unimodular triangulations or introduce new partial orders on the permutations. Sanyal and Stump
[
32
, §5]
extended Li’s results to
P
-hypersimplices
. More recently, Jiang
[
22
]
, Bullock and Jiang
[
8
]
, have studied the Ehrhart theory of positroids and general alcoved polytopes, specifically in the case where
\Lambda
is the integer lattice. Since the vertices of alcoved polytopes
P
are only guaranteed to be rational, the functions
L^{\Lambda}_{P}
are, in general, only quasi-polynomials.

2.
Background

We introduce the following notation that will be used throughout the document.
For a positive integer
n
, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[n]}:=\{1,\dots,n\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}[\![n]\!]}:=\{-n,\dots,-1,1,\dots,n\}
. For brevity, we also write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\overline{i}}:=-i
for all integers
i
. Similarly, for
i\in[n]
, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}e_{\overline{i}}}:=-e_{i}
, where
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
denotes the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

2.1.
Signed permutations

A
signed permutation
of length
n
is a permutation
w
of the set
[\![n]\!]
that satisfies
w(\overline{i})=\overline{w(i)}
for all
i\in[\![n]\!]
.
Under composition, they form the
hyperoctahedral group
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathfrak{B}_{n}}
.
This is the finite Coxeter group of type
B_{n}
or
C_{n}
.

The
window notation
of a signed permutation
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is the word
w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n}\in[\![n]\!]^{n}
, where
w_{i}=w(i)
. We usually identify
w
with its window notation and write
w=w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n}
.
On the other hand, the
complete notation
of
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
is the word

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widetilde{w}}:=w_{\overline{n}}\cdots w_{\overline{1}}w_{1}\cdots w_{n},

where
w_{\overline{i}}=\overline{w_{i}}=w(\overline{i})
for all
i\in[\![n]\!]
.

2.1.1.
Signed permutation statistics and type B Eulerian numbers

We review some well-studied statistics on signed permutations.

For a signed permutation
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, a
descent
is a position
i\in[0,n-1]
such that
w_{i}>w_{i+1}
, where by convention
w_{0}:=0
. We let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\des_{B}(w)}
denote the number of descents of
w
. The statistic
\des_{B}
is precisely the descent statistic of
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
as a Coxeter group; see, for instance,
[
30
, Chapter 13]
. The
type B Eulerian numbers
enumerate signed permutations by the descent statistic; namely, for
0\leq k\leq n
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}B_{n,k}}:=|\{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\mid\des_{B}(w)=k\}|.

The
flag-descent
statistic, introduced by Adin, Brenti, and Roichman in
[
2
]
, is a variation of the descent statistic, defined as:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\fdes(w)}:=2\des_{B}(w)-\delta_{w_{1}<0},

where
\delta_{w_{1}<0}\in\{0,1\}
equals
1
if and only if
w_{1}<0
. Crucially, and directly from this definition, we have that

(8)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:desBandfdes}\des_{B}(w)=\left\lfloor\tfrac{\fdes(w)+1}{2}\right\rfloor,

for all
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

Additionally, we say
i\in[n]
is an
excedance
(respectively,
negation
) of
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
if
w_{i}>i
(respectively,
w_{i}<0
). We denote the number of excedances and negations of
w
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\exc(w)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\fneg(w)}
, respectively. The
flag-excedance
statistic, introduced by Foata and Han in
[
16
]
is defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\fexc(w)}:=2\exc(w)+\fneg(w).

In the same article, they show that the statistics
\fdes
and
\fexc
have the same distribution.
Consequently, the statistic

(9)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:excB}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\exc_{B}(w)}:=\left\lfloor\tfrac{\fexc(w)+1}{2}\right\rfloor,

introduced by the second author in
[
3
]
, is Eulerian (has the same distribution as
\des_{B}
.)

2.2.
Alcoved polytopes

This section provides an overview of key aspects of the work by Lam and Postnikov in
[
25
,
26
]
that are central to the discussion in this paper. We assume some familiarity with the theory of Coxeter groups and root systems. For terminology, we refer the reader to the books by Bourbaki
[
6
]
and by Humphreys
[
20
]
.

2.2.1.
Root systems

Consider an
n
-dimensional real Euclidean space
V
with a nondegenerate inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Let
\Phi\subset V
be an irreducible crystallographic root system with a fixed set of
simple roots

\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\}\subset\Phi
. Denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Phi^{+}}:=\Phi\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\}
its set of
positive roots
.
The
root order
is the partial order on
\Phi^{+}
determined by
\alpha\leq\beta
if and only if
\beta-\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\}
. It has a unique maximum element
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\theta}
, called the
highest root
of
\Phi
. Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be the constants such that

(10)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:highest}\theta=a_{1}\alpha_{1}+\dots+a_{n}\alpha_{n}.

The
Coxeter number
of
\Phi
is
h(\Phi):=a_{1}+\dots+a_{n}+1
. Finally, the
fundamental coweights

\{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n}\}
form the basis of
V
dual to the simple roots, so
a_{i}=\langle\theta,\omega_{i}\rangle
.

Example 2

(Type A)
.

Let
V=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}/\mathbb{R}{\bf 1}
, where
{\bf 1}
denotes the all-ones vector.
The root system
\Phi\subset V
of
type
A_{n}
has simple roots

\alpha_{1}=e_{2}-e_{1},\quad\alpha_{2}=e_{3}-e_{2},\quad\dots\quad\alpha_{n-1}=e_{n}-e_{n-1},\quad\alpha_{n}=e_{n+1}-e_{n}.

Its set of positive roots and its highest root are respectively

\Phi^{+}=\{e_{j}-e_{i}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n+1\}\quad\text{and}\quad\theta=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\dots+\alpha_{n}=e_{n+1}-e_{1}.

Lastly, its fundamental coweights are (the projections to
V
of)

\omega_{1}=e_{2}+e_{3}+\dots+e_{n+1},\quad\omega_{2}=e_{3}+\dots+e_{n+1},\quad\dots\quad\omega_{n}=e_{n+1}.

Example 3

(Type C)
.

Let
V=\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
The root system
\Phi\subset V
of
type
C_{n}
has simple roots

\alpha_{1}=2e_{1},\quad\alpha_{2}=e_{2}-e_{1},\quad\dots\quad\alpha_{n-1}=e_{n-1}-e_{n-2},\quad\alpha_{n}=e_{n}-e_{n-1}.

Its set of positive roots and its highest root are respectively

\Phi^{+}=\{e_{j}\pm e_{i}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n\}\cup\{2e_{i}\mid i\in[n]\}\quad\text{and}\quad\theta=\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\dots+2\alpha_{n-1}+2\alpha_{n}=2e_{n}.

Figure

1
shows the positive roots for
n=2
.
Lastly, its fundamental coweights are

\omega_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(e_{1}+e_{2}+\dots+e_{n}\right),\quad\omega_{2}=e_{2}+e_{3}+\dots+e_{n},\quad\omega_{3}=e_{3}+\dots+e_{n},\quad\dots\quad\omega_{n}=e_{n}.

2.2.2.
Affine Coxeter arrangement and alcoves

The
affine Coxeter arrangement
associated with
\Phi
is the collection of hyperplanes

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}}:=\{H_{\alpha,k}\mid\alpha\in\Phi^{+},k\in\mathbb{Z}\},\qquad\text{where}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}H_{\alpha,k}}:=\{x\in V\mid\langle x,\alpha\rangle=k\}.

The
affine Coxeter group

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widetilde{W}_{\Phi}}
is the group of affine transformations of
V
generated by reflections across the hyperplanes in
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
.
The
finite Coxeter group

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}W_{\Phi}}
is the subgroup of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widetilde{W}_{\Phi}}
generated by reflections across the linear hyperplanes
H_{\alpha,0}
.
See
Figure

1
for a picture of
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
when
\Phi
is of type
C_{2}
.

\Phi^{+}_{C_{2}}=\begin{gathered}\hbox to105.91pt{\vbox to82.8pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.41585pt\lower-34.34364pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{
{\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}
{}{}{}

Figure 1
.

Left: Positive roots of type
C_{2}
.
Right: The affine arrangement
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi_{C_{2}}}

with the fundamental parallelepiped
\Pi_{C_{2}}
subdivided into the three hypersimplices
\Delta_{C_{2},1}
(red, bottom),
\Delta_{C_{2},2}
(blue, center), and
\Delta_{C_{2},3}
(orange, top).
Only thick or dashed hyperplanes are labeled.

The complement in
V
of the hyperplanes in
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
is the disjoint union of bounded convex open sets, with their closures being the
alcoves
of the arrangement. The
fundamental alcove
is

(11)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:fund-alcove}\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A_{\circ}}:=&\{x\in V\mid 0\leq\langle x,\alpha\rangle\leq 1\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\}\\
=&{\rm Conv}\{0,\tfrac{\omega_{1}}{a_{1}},\dots,\tfrac{\omega_{n}}{a_{n}}\}.\end{split}

Let
\leq
denote the
weak order
on the alcoves of
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
with base region
A_{\circ}

[
29
,
11
]
.
That is,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A\leq A^{\prime}}
if and only if every hyperplane
H\in\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
separating
A_{\circ}
and
A
also separates
A_{\circ}
and
A^{\prime}
. The group
{\widetilde{W}_{\Phi}}
acts faithfully and transitively on the collection of alcoves, allowing us to identify elements
w\in\widetilde{W}_{\Phi}
with alcoves
w\cdot A_{\circ}
. Under this identification, the weak order on alcoves and the right weak order on
\widetilde{W}_{\Phi}
as a Coxeter group agree.

The
fundamental parallelepiped
is the polytope

(12)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-fund_{p}arall}\begin{split}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Pi_{\Phi}}:=&\{x\in V\mid 0\leq\langle x,\alpha_{i}\rangle\leq 1\text{ for all }i=1,\dots,n\}\\
=&[0,1]\omega_{1}+[0,1]\omega_{2}+\dots+[0,1]\omega_{n},\end{split}

where
[0,1]\omega_{i}\subset V
is the segment
\{\lambda\omega_{i}\mid 0\leq\lambda\leq 1\}
and the sum denotes Minkowski sum:

A+B=\{a+b\mid a\in A,\,b\in B\}.

It is bounded by hyperplanes of the affine arrangement
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
, and is therefore a union of its alcoves. In fact, it contains exactly

(13)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:volPi}\Vol(\Pi_{\Phi})/\Vol(A_{\circ})=n!\cdot a_{1}\dots a_{n}

alcoves, where
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
are the constants in
Equation

10
. Moreover, the alcoves contained in
\Pi_{\Phi}
form an interval in the weak order with minimum element
A_{\circ}
. We let

(14)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:defI}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{I}_{\Phi}}:=\{A\text{ alcove }\mid A\subset\Pi_{\Phi}\}

denote this poset and

(15)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-Psi}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Psi_{\Phi}(t)}:=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{I}_{\Phi}}t^{{\rm cover}(A)}

denote the polynomial whose coefficients keep the distribution of the number of lower covers of elements in
\mathcal{I}_{\Phi}
. When
\Phi
is of type A,
\Psi_{A_{n}}(t)=E_{A_{n-1}}(t)
, the Eulerian polynomial; see for instance
[
1
, Theorem 27]
. In
[
17
]
, Gashi, Schedler, and Speyer compute the Hilbert polynomial
\mathcal{I}_{\Phi}
for the root systems of types A, B, C, and D.

2.2.3.
Alcoved polytopes and generalized hypersimplices

Every alcove is determined by a set of inequalities
k_{\alpha}\leq\langle x,\alpha\rangle\leq k_{\alpha}+1
for some vector of integers
(k_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Phi^{+}}
.
If we relax the constraints on the integers
k_{\alpha}
and
k_{\alpha}+1
being consecutive, we obtain a larger class of polytopes.

Definition 4

(Lam–Postnikov)
.

A polytope
P\subset V
is called an
alcoved polytope
if it is defined by inequalities
a_{\alpha}\leq\langle x,\alpha\rangle\leq b_{\alpha}
for some collection of integers
1
1

1

In their later work, Lam and Postnikov extend the definition of alcoved polytopes to include non-integer inequalities; see, for instance,
[
27
]
. However, since we are interested in the
alcove triangulation
of these polytopes, we focus on the integer case.

\{a_{\alpha},b_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}
.

If
P
is a full-dimensional polytope, then
P
is an alcoved polytope if and only if
P
is the union of some alcoves of
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
. This family of polytopes naturally includes the alcoves of
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
and the fundamental parallelepiped in
Equation

12
. Another important family of alcoved polytopes can be obtained by slicing the fundamental parallelepiped with the hyperplanes of
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
orthogonal to the highest root
\theta
.

Definition 5

(Lam–Postnikov)
.

The generalized hypersimplices, or
\Phi
-hypersimplices
, are the polytopes

(16)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-hyper}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Delta_{\Phi,k}}:=\{x\in\Pi_{\Phi}\mid k-1\leq\langle x,\theta\rangle\leq k\},\quad\text{for }k=1,\dots,h(\Phi)-1.

See
Figure

1
for a picture of all
\Phi
-hypersimplices when the root system is of type
C_{2}
.
When
\Phi
is the root system of type
A_{n-1}
, the hypersimplex
\Delta_{\Phi,k}
is integrally equivalent to the usual hypersimplex
\Delta_{n,k}
discussed in the
1.
Introduction
, whose volume is determined by the distribution of the descent statistic on
\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
. More generally, the volume of the
\Phi
-hypersimplices
is determined by the circular descent statistic
\cdes
described below.

Set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\alpha_{0}}:=-\theta
, and for each
w\in W_{\Phi}
and
i=0,1,\dots,n
let

d_{i}(w)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }w(\alpha_{i})\in\Phi^{+},\\
1&\text{if }w(\alpha_{i})\in\Phi^{-}.\end{cases}

Definition 6

(Lam–Postnikov)
.

The
circular descent number
of
w
is

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\cdes(w)}:=d_{0}(w)+a_{1}d_{1}(w)+a_{2}d_{2}(w)+\dots+a_{n}d_{n}(w),

where
\{a_{i}\}_{i}
are the integers such that
\theta=a_{1}\alpha_{1}+\dots+a_{n}\alpha_{n}
.

Recall that the
index of connection
is the constant
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}f}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
|W_{\Phi}|=f\cdot n!\cdot a_{1}\dots a_{n}
. The following formula for the volume of the
\Phi
-hypersimplices is
[
26
, Theorem 9.3]
.

(17)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:LP-volume}\Vol(\Delta_{\Phi,k})=\frac{1}{f}\left|\{w\in W_{\Phi}\mid\cdes(w)=k\}\right|.

3.
Type C hypersimplices

For the rest of the document, we will focus on the case where
\Phi=\Phi_{C_{n}}
is the root system of type
C_{n}
in
Example

3
. Recall that its simple roots are

\alpha_{1}=2e_{1},\quad\alpha_{2}=e_{2}-e_{1},\quad\dots,\quad\alpha_{n-1}=e_{n-1}-e_{n-2},\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha_{n}=e_{n}-e_{n-1}.

The corresponding set of positive roots is

\Phi^{+}=\{e_{j}\pm e_{i}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq n\}\cup\{2e_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\},

and the highest root is

\theta=1\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\dots+2\alpha_{n-1}+2\alpha_{n}=2e_{n}.

Hence,
h(\Phi_{C_{n}})=1+2+\dots+2+1=2n
.
Its fundamental coweights are

\omega_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{1}+e_{2}+\dots+e_{n}),\quad\omega_{2}=e_{2}+\dots+e_{n},\quad\dots,\quad\omega_{n-1}=e_{n-1}+e_{n},\quad\omega_{n}=e_{n}.

Therefore, the lattice generated by the vertices of
A_{\circ}
is
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}\{\frac{\omega_{i}}{a_{i}}\}_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
; which contains the vertices of all the alcoves of
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi_{C_{n}}}
.
Every alcove is thus
unimodular
with respect to
\Lambda
, in particular they all have normalized volume
1
. This also occurs in type A, but not in type B or D; see
Section

10
.
The associated finite Coxeter group is the hyperoctahedral group
W_{\Phi_{C}}\cong\mathfrak{B}_{n}
reviewed in
Section

2.1
.

The
type C fundamental parallelepiped
\Pi_{C_{n}}
is the polytope

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Pi_{C_{n}}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid 0\leq 2x_{1},x_{2}-x_{1},\dots,x_{n}-x_{n-1}\leq 1\}.

For
1\leq k\leq 2n-1
, the
type C hypersimplex
\Delta_{C_{n},k}
is

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Delta_{C_{n},k}}:=\{x\in\Pi_{n}\mid k-1\leq 2x_{n}\leq k\}.

Definition 7
.

The
half-open type C hypersimplices
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}
are defined by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},1}}:=\{x\in\Pi_{C_{n}}\mid 0\leq 2x_{n}\leq 1\}=\Delta_{C_{n},1},

and, for
2\leq k\leq 2n-1
,

(18)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-half-open}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}:=\{x\in\Pi_{C_{n}}\mid k-1<2x_{n}\leq k\}=\Delta_{C_{n},k}\setminus(\Delta_{C_{n},k}\cap\Delta_{C_{n},k-1}).

4.
Proof of Theorem
B

We prove
Theorem

B
using generating functions and adapting techniques of Li
[
28
]
to our context. In particular, we need to understand the generating function for:
i.
the joint distribution of the statistics
\fexc
and
\des_{B}
on the sets
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
of signed permutations
w
satisfying
w^{-1}(1)>0
(see
Definition

1
), and
ii.
the values of the Ehrhart polynomial of all type C half-open hypersimplices. We achieve these objectives in the following two propositions, respectively.

Proposition 8
.

The following identity holds.

(19)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:p-aux1}\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{w\in X_{n}}s^{\fexc(w)}t^{\des_{B}(w)}\dfrac{u^{n}}{(1-t)^{n+1}}=\dfrac{1}{(1+s)}\sum_{r\geq 0}\dfrac{(1-us^{2})^{r+1}-(1-u)^{r+1}}{(1-u)^{r+1}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}}t^{r}.

Proposition 9
.

For every
r\geq 0
,

(20)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:p-aux2}\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{k\geq 0}L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k+1}}(r)s^{k}u^{n}=\dfrac{(1-us^{2})^{r+1}-(1-u)^{r+1}}{(1+s)\big((1-u)^{r+1}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}\big)}.

We give a complete proof of these two results below, but we first use them to prove one of our main results.

Proof of
Theorem

B
.

Multiply both sides of
Equation

20
by
t^{r}
and sum over all
r\geq 0
.
The resulting right-hand side is exactly the right-hand side of
Equation

19
. Therefore,

\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{r\geq 0}L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k+1}}(r)t^{r}s^{k}u^{n}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{w\in X_{n}}s^{\fexc(w)}t^{\des_{B}(w)}\dfrac{u^{n}}{(1-t)^{n+1}}.

Extracting the coefficient of
s^{k-1}u^{n}
on both sides of this equation, we obtain

\sum_{r\geq 0}L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}(r)t^{r}=(1-t)^{-(n+1)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in X_{n}\\
\fexc(w)=k-1\end{subarray}}t^{\des_{B}(w)}.

The result now follows by the definition of the
h^{*}
-polynomial (
Equation

1
) and the fact that
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}
has dimension
n
.
∎

We now proceed to prove the two propositions in detail.

Proof of
Proposition

8
.

Foata and Han
[
16
, Equation (9.3)]
show that

\sum_{n\geq 0}\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}s^{\fexc(w)}t^{\fdes(w)}\dfrac{u^{n}}{(1-t^{2})^{n}}=\\
\sum_{r\geq 0}\bigg(\dfrac{(1-s)(1-t)t^{2r}}{(1-u)^{r+1}(1-us^{2})^{-r}-s(1-u)}-\dfrac{(1-s)(1-t)t^{2r+1}}{(1-u)^{r+1}(1-us^{2})^{-r}-s(1-us)}\bigg).

We will first manipulate this expression to replace the statistic
\fdes
with
\des_{B}
, and then to restrict the domain of the internal sum from all signed permutations to those for which
w^{-1}(1)>0
.

Recall from
Equation

8
that
\des_{B}(w)=\lfloor\tfrac{\fdes(w)+1}{2}\rfloor
for all
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.
Since the factors
\frac{1}{(1-t^{2})^{n}}
only involve even powers of
t
,
we can expand the expression above in powers of
t
and substitute
t^{2k-1},t^{2k}\mapsto t^{k}

to obtain the generating function for the statistics
(\fexc,\des_{B})
.
For the two terms inside the sum of the right-hand side, these substitutions are, respectively,

\displaystyle(1-t)t^{2r}=t^{2r}-t^{2r+1}

\displaystyle\mapsto t^{r}-t^{r+1}=(1-t)t^{r},

and

\displaystyle(1-t)t^{2r+1}=t^{2r+1}-t^{2r+2}

\displaystyle\mapsto t^{r+1}-t^{r+1}=0.

Therefore, the generating function for the joint distribution of
(\fexc,\des_{B})
is

(21)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:FH}\sum_{n\geq 0}\sum_{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}}s^{\fexc(w)}t^{\des_{B}(w)}\dfrac{u^{n}}{(1-t)^{n+1}}=\sum_{r\geq 0}\dfrac{(1-s)(1-us^{2})^{r}t^{r}}{(1-u)^{r+1}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}}.

Notice that we have divided both sides by
(1-t)
and multiplied the right-hand side by
1=\frac{(1-us^{2})^{r}}{(1-us^{2})^{r}}
.

Now, for
n\geq 1
and
k\in[\![n]\!]
, let

B^{(k)}_{n}(s,t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\\
w^{-1}(1)=k\end{subarray}}s^{\fexc(w)}t^{\des_{B}(w)}.

Also, let
B_{0}(s,t)=1
and for
n>0
,

B_{n}(s,t)=\sum_{k\in[\![n]\!]}B^{(k)}_{n}(s,t),\qquad\text{and}\qquad B^{>1}_{n}(s,t)=\sum_{k>1}B^{(k)}_{n}(s,t).

Thus,
21
is the ordinary generating function of
\frac{B_{n}(s,t)}{(1-t)^{n+1}}
,
and we want to compute the ordinary generating function of
\frac{B^{(1)}_{n}(s,t)+B^{>1}_{n}(s,t)}{(1-t)^{n+1}}
.

Observe that:

•

If
w=1w_{2}\dots w_{n}
and
u
is the standardization of
w_{2}\dots w_{n}

(that is,
|u_{i}|=|w_{i+1}|-1
and
u_{i}w_{i+1}>0
),
then
\fexc(u)=\fexc(w)
and
\des_{B}(u)=\des_{B}(w)
.
Indeed,
1
was neither an excedance nor a negation of
w
and
i
is an excedance (resp. negation) of
u
if and only if
i+1
is an excedance (resp. negation) of
w
. Similarly, since
w_{1}>0
,
0
is not a descent of
w
and
i\in[0,n-2]
is a descent of
u
if and only if
i+1
is a descent of
w
.
Thus,

B^{(1)}_{n}(s,t)=B_{n-1}(s,t).

•

If
w=1w_{2}\dots w_{n}
and
u=\overline{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n}
, then

\fexc(u)=\fexc(w)+1
(since
1
is now a negation of
u
) and

\des_{B}(u)=\des_{B}(w)+1
(since
0
is now a descent of
u
).
Thus,

B^{(-1)}_{n}(s,t)=stB^{(1)}_{n}(s,t).

•

If
w^{-1}(1)=k>1
and
u
is obtained from
w
by negating
w_{k}=1
, then

\fexc(u)=\fexc(w)+1
(since
k
is now a negation of
u
) and

\des_{B}(u)=\des_{B}(w)
(since
w_{k-1}
and
w_{k+1}
are either both larger than
1
and than
-1
, or both smaller than
1
and than
-1
).
Thus,

B^{(-k)}_{n}(s,t)=sB^{(k)}_{n}(s,t),\qquad\text{for all }k>1.

Therefore,
B_{n}(s,t)=(1+st)B_{n-1}(s,t)+(1+s)B^{>1}_{n}(s,t)
, and

B^{(1)}_{n}(s,t)+B^{>1}_{n}(s,t)=B_{n-1}(s,t)+\dfrac{B_{n}(s,t)-(1+st)B_{n-1}(s,t)}{(1+s)}=\dfrac{B_{n}(s,t)+s(1-t)B_{n-1}(s,t)}{(1+s)}.

Using the generating function for
\frac{B_{n}(s,t)}{(1-t)^{n+1}}
in
Equation

21
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 1}(B^{(1)}_{n}(s,t)+B^{>1}_{n}(s,t))\dfrac{u^{n}}{(1-t)^{n+1}}=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{(1+s)}\left(\sum_{n\geq 1}B_{n}(s,t)\dfrac{u^{n}}{(1-t)^{n+1}}+su\sum_{n\geq 1}B_{n-1}(s,t)\dfrac{u^{n-1}}{(1-t)^{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{(1+s)}\left(\sum_{r\geq 0}\dfrac{(1+su)(1-s)(1-us^{2})^{r}t^{r}}{(1-u)^{r+1}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}}-\dfrac{1}{(1-t)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{(1+s)}\sum_{r\geq 0}\bigg(\dfrac{(1+su)(1-s)(1-us^{2})^{r}}{(1-u)^{r+1}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}}-1\bigg)t^{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{(1+s)}\sum_{r\geq 0}\dfrac{(1-us^{2})^{r+1}-(1-u)^{r+1}}{(1-u)^{r+1}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}}t^{r},

where in the last step we use that

(1+us)(1-s)(1-us^{2})^{r}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}=(1-us^{2})^{r+1}.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

9
.

Recall that the hypersimplex
\Delta_{C_{n},k+1}
is determined by inequalities

0\leq 2x_{1}\,,\,x_{2}-x_{1}\,,\,\dots\,,\,x_{n}-x_{n-1}\leq 1\quad\text{ and }\quad k\leq 2x_{n}\leq k+1.

Under the change of coordinates

y_{1}=2x_{1},\ y_{2}=2(x_{2}-x_{1}),\ \dots,\ \ y_{n}=2(x_{n}-x_{n-1}),

counting half-integer points in dilations of
\Delta_{C_{n},k+1}

corresponds to counting integer points inside dilations of

\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid 0\leq y_{1}\leq 1\,,\>\>0\leq y_{2},\dots,y_{n}\leq 2\,,\>\>k\leq y_{1}+\dots+y_{n}\leq k+1\}.

Therefore, for any
k>0
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k+1}}(r)=\Big|\{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\mid 0\leq y_{1}\leq r\,,\>\>0\leq y_{2},\dots,y_{n}\leq 2r\,,\>\>kr<y_{1}+\dots+y_{n}\leq(k+1)r\}\Big|.

For
k=0
, we need to add
1
to the formula above to account for the point
(0,\dots,0)\in\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},1}=\Delta_{C_{n},1}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k+1}}(r)=

\displaystyle\delta_{k,0}+([x^{kr+1}]+\cdots+[x^{(k+1)r}])\left(\frac{1-x^{r+1}}{1-x}\right)\left(\frac{1-x^{2r+1}}{1-x}\right)^{n-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{k,0}+[x^{kr}](x^{-1}+\cdots+x^{-r})\left(\frac{1-x^{r+1}}{1-x}\right)\left(\frac{1-x^{2r+1}}{1-x}\right)^{n-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{k,0}+[x^{kr}]\frac{(1-x^{r})(1-x^{r+1})(1-x^{2r+1})^{n-1}}{(1-x)^{n+1}x^{r}},

where
\delta_{k,0}
denotes the Kronecker delta and
[x^{j}]f(x)
denotes the coefficient of
x^{j}
in the power series expansion of
f(x)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 1}L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k+1}}(r)u^{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 1}\delta_{k,0}u^{n}+\sum_{n\geq 1}[x^{kr}]\frac{(1-x^{r})(1-x^{r+1})(1-x^{2r+1})^{n-1}}{(1-x)^{n+1}x^{r}}u^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{k,0}\dfrac{u}{1-u}+[x^{kr}]\frac{(1-x^{r})(1-x^{r+1})u}{(1-x)^{2}x^{r}}\sum_{n\geq 1}\left(\frac{(1-x^{2r+1})u}{1-x}\right)^{n-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{k,0}\dfrac{u}{1-u}+[x^{kr}]\frac{(1-x^{r})(1-x^{r+1})u}{(1-x)x^{r}(1-x-u+ux^{2r+1})}.

So,

\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{k\geq 0}L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k+1}}(r)s^{k}u^{n}=\dfrac{u}{1-u}+\sum_{k\geq 0}s^{k}[x^{kr}]\frac{(1-x^{r})(1-x^{r+1})u}{(1-x)x^{r}(1-x-u+ux^{2r+1})}.

Let
z=\frac{1-ux^{2r}}{1-u}
.
We have,

(22)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:long-proof-aux1}\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{k\geq 0}L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k+1}}(r)s^{k}u^{n}=\dfrac{u}{1-u}+\sum_{k\geq 0}s^{k}[x^{kr}]\frac{(1-x^{r})(1-x^{r+1})u}{x^{r}(1-x)(1-u)(1-zx)}.

Since

\sum_{k\geq 0}s^{k}[x^{kr}]x^{m}=\begin{cases}s^{j}&\text{if }m=rj\text{ for some }j,\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

we can drop all the terms of the form
c_{m}x^{m}
where
m
is not a multiple of
r
in the expansion of
\frac{(1-x^{r})(1-x^{r+1})u}{x^{r}(1-x)(1-u)(1-zx)}
above.
Hence, we can replace the factor

\displaystyle\frac{(1-x^{r+1})}{x^{r}(1-x)(1-zx)}=

\displaystyle(x^{-r}+x)\Big(\sum_{i\geq 0}x^{i}\Big)\Big(\sum_{j\geq 0}(zx)^{j}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(x^{-r}+x)\sum_{m\geq 0}(1+z+\dots+z^{m})x^{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m\geq 0}(1+z+\dots+z^{m})x^{m-r}-\sum_{m\geq 0}(1+z+\dots+z^{m})x^{m+1}

on the right-hand side
Equation

22
with

\displaystyle x^{-r}+\sum_{j\geq 1}(1+z+\dots+z^{rj})x^{rj-r}-\sum_{j\geq 1}(1+z+\dots+z^{rj-1})x^{rj}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{-r}+\sum_{j\geq 0}(z^{rj}+z^{rj+1}+\dots+z^{r(j+1)})x^{rj}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{-r}+\sum_{j\geq 0}(1+z^{1}+\dots+z^{r})z^{rj}x^{rj}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{-r}+\dfrac{1-z^{r+1}}{1-z}\cdot\dfrac{1}{1-z^{r}x^{r}}.

The contribution of the term
x^{-r}
above to the right-hand side of
Equation

22
is

\sum_{k\geq 0}s^{k}[x^{kr}]\frac{(1-x^{r})u}{(1-u)}x^{-r}=\dfrac{u}{1-u}\sum_{k\geq 0}s^{k}[x^{kr}](x^{-r}-1)=-\dfrac{u}{1-u}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{k\geq 0}L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k+1}}(r)s^{k}u^{n}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}s^{k}[x^{kr}]\frac{(1-x^{r})(1-z^{r+1})u}{(1-z)(1-u)(1-z^{r}x^{r})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-s)\left(1-\left(\tfrac{1-us^{2}}{1-u}\right)^{r+1}\right)u}{\left(1-\left(\tfrac{1-us^{2}}{1-u}\right)\right)(1-u)\left(1-\left(\tfrac{1-us^{2}}{1-u}\right)^{r}s\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-s)\left((1-u)^{r+1}-(1-us^{2})^{r+1}\right)u}{\left((1-u)-(1-us^{2})\right)(1-u)\left((1-u)^{r}-(1-us^{2})^{r}s\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-s)((1-u)^{r+1}-(1-us^{2})^{r+1})}{(s^{2}-1)\left((1-u)^{r+1}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(1-us^{2})^{r+1}-(1-u)^{r+1}}{(1+s)\left((1-u)^{r+1}-s(1-u)(1-us^{2})^{r}\right)}\qed

5.
Combinatorial Model: Poset on Signed Permutations

Using a novel statistic on signed permutations, we develop a combinatorial model for the poset
\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
of alcoves in the fundamental parallelepiped introduced in
Equation

14
. We then use this model to prove
Theorem

A
.

5.1.
Big ascents

Endow
[\![n]\!]
with the order induced from
\mathbb{Z}
:

\overline{n}<\dots<\overline{2}<\overline{1}<1<2<\dots<n.

We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}i\ll j}
to indicate that
i<k<j
for some
k\in[\![n]\!]
. For instance,
4\ll 8
and
\overline{2}\ll 1
, but
\overline{1}\not\ll 1
. We use
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}i^{+}}
to denote the
cyclic successor
of
i\in[\![n]\!]
in the order above. For example,
\overline{1}^{+}=1
,
2^{+}=3
, and
n^{+}=\overline{n}
.

Definition 10
.

We say that a signed permutation
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
has a
big ascent
at position
i\in\{\overline{1}\}\cup[n]
if
w_{i}\ll w_{i^{+}}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\BAsc(w)}
denote the set of big ascents of
w
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\basc(w)}:=|\BAsc(w)|
. Thus,

\displaystyle\overline{1}\in\BAsc(w)

\displaystyle\quad\text{if and only if}\quad w_{1}\geq 2,

\displaystyle\text{for }i\in[n-1],\,i\in\BAsc(w)

\displaystyle\quad\text{if and only if}\quad w_{i}\ll w_{i+1},

\displaystyle n\in\BAsc(w)

\displaystyle\quad\text{if and only if}\quad w_{n}\leq\overline{2}.

See
Figure

2
for many examples of big ascents.

Observe that
\BAsc(w)
is always a proper subset of
\{\overline{1}\}\cup[n]
; otherwise, the inequalities

\overline{w_{1}}\ll w_{1}\ll\dots\ll w_{n}\ll\overline{w_{n}},

would imply the existence of a strict chain of length
(n+2)+(n+1)
within
[\![n]\!]
. We invite the reader to verify that, in fact,
0\leq\basc(w)\leq n-1
for all signed permutations
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, and that the minimum value is attained by exactly two signed permutations.

Remark 11
.

When all the entries in the window notation of
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
are positive, the statistic
\basc(w)
is precisely the big ascent statistic for unsigned permutations that appears in the work of Sanyal and Stump
[
32
]
.

5.2.
Poset structure

Recall from
Definition

1
that
X_{n}
denotes the collection of signed permutations
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
for which
w^{-1}(1)>0
.

Definition 12
.

Given
u,w\in X_{n}
with
w=w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n-1}w_{n}
,
we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}u\rightharpoonup w}
if one of the following three conditions holds:

(23)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:cover-rel}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}w_{1}\geq 2&\text{and}&u={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overline{w_{1}}}w_{2}\dots w_{n-1}w_{n};\text{ or}\\
i\in\cap[n-1],\,w_{i}\ll w_{i+1}&\text{and}&u=w_{1}\dots w_{i-1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}w_{i+1}w_{i}}w_{i+2}\dots w_{n};\text{ or}\\
w_{n}\leq\overline{2}&\text{and}&u=w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n-1}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\overline{w_{n}}}.\end{array}\right.

Thus, for each
w\in X_{n}
, there are exactly
\basc(w)
signed permutations
u\in X_{n}
such that
u\rightharpoonup w
.

Example 13
.

Below we display three instances of the relation
\rightharpoonup
in
X_{4}
.

\displaystyle\hbox{\pagecolor{RoyalBlue!20}$\displaystyle\overline{3}$}1\,\overline{4}\,2\rightharpoonup\hbox{\pagecolor{RoyalBlue!20}$\displaystyle 3$}1\,\overline{4}\,2,\hskip 51.7479pt\overline{3}\hbox{\pagecolor{RoyalBlue!20}$\displaystyle 1\,\overline{4}$}2\rightharpoonup\overline{3}\hbox{\pagecolor{RoyalBlue!20}$\displaystyle\overline{4}\,1$}2,\hskip 51.7479pt\overline{3}\,\overline{4}\,1\hbox{\pagecolor{RoyalBlue!20}$\displaystyle 2$}\rightharpoonup\overline{3}\,\overline{4}\,1\hbox{\pagecolor{RoyalBlue!20}$\displaystyle\overline{2}$}.

The permutations on the right have big ascents at positions
\overline{1}
,
2
, and
4
, respectively.

Theorem 14
.

The relation
\rightharpoonup

above
is the covering relation of a partial order
\leq
on
X_{n}
.
Moreover, there is an isomorphism
A:X_{n}\to\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
such that, for all
w\in X_{n}
,
A(w)
lies inside the hypersimplex
\Delta_{C_{n},\cdes(w^{-1})}
.

We prove
Theorem

14
in
Section

5.5
. In the following section, we use this result to prove
Theorem

A
.

Figure 2
.

The poset
(X_{n},\leq)
for
n=2
(left) and
n=3
(right) with the big ascent positions
i\in\{\overline{1}\}\cup[n]

underlined with a little wedge “
\widehat{\phantom{a}}
”.
Elements
w\in X_{n}
are grouped by the value of
\cdes(w^{-1})
.

5.3.
Proof of Theorem
A

Recall that we want to prove that for all
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
,

h_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}^{*}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in X_{n}\\
\cdes(w^{-1})=k\end{subarray}}t^{\basc(w)}.

To achieve this, we use the following result of Stanley regarding the
h^{*}
-polynomial of polytopes with a
shellable unimodular triangulation
.

Let
P
be a polytope with a unimodular triangulation
\Gamma
. Suppose there exists a linear ordering
2
2

2

Such an ordering is called a
shelling
of
\Gamma
, and it does not necessarily exist.

T_{1}\prec T_{2}\prec\dots
of the maximal simplices in
\Gamma
such that the intersection of
T_{k}
with
T_{1}\cup\dots\cup T_{k-1}
is a union of facets of
T_{k}
. Let
c(T_{k})
be the number of facets of
T_{k}
in this intersection. Then, a result of Stanley
[
33
]
shows that

(24)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:Stanley-shelling}h^{*}_{P}(t)=\sum_{i}t^{c(T_{i})}.

Proof of
Theorem

A
.

For
n\geq 1
and
1\leq k\leq 2n-1
, let
P_{n,k}=\{x\in\Pi_{C_{n}}\mid 2x_{1}\leq k\}
.
The polytope
P_{n,k}
is the disjoint union of
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},1},\dots,\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}
, and so

L_{P_{n,k}}(r)=L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},1}}(r)+\dots+L_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}(r)
.
Moreover, since all these polytopes have the same dimension,

h^{*}_{P_{n,k}}(t)=h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},1}}(t)+\dots+h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}(t).

The collection
\mathcal{P}_{n,k}
of alcoves contained in
P_{n,k}
forms an order ideal of
\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
, since the hyperplane
H_{\theta,k}
separates the fundamental alcove
A_{\circ}
from every alcove of
\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
that is not in
\mathcal{P}_{n,k}
, and no others.
Fix any linear extension
\prec
of
\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
such that alcoves in
\mathcal{P}_{n,k}
appear before those in
\mathcal{P}_{n,k+1}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{n,k}
.
Since every linear extension of the weak order on a hyperplane arrangement

3
3

3

We can restrict to a finite linear arrangement by forgetting
the hyperplanes not intersecting
\Pi_{C_{n}}
and taking the cone over them.

gives a shelling of the complex of regions (see for instance
[
31
, Proposition 3.4]
),

\prec
defines a shelling on the alcove triangulation of each
P_{n,k}
.
Moreover, the number of facets
c(A)
of an alcove
A
that appear in the intersection with the preceding alcoves is precisely the number of elements covered by
A
in the weak order.
Therefore, by
Theorem

14
and Stanley’s formula
24
, we have

(25)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:h-star-Pnk}h^{*}_{P_{n,k}}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in X_{n}:\\
\cdes(w^{-1})\leq k\end{subarray}}t^{\basc(w)},

and therefore

h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}(t)=h^{*}_{P_{n,k}}(t)-h^{*}_{P_{n,k-1}}(t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in X_{n}:\\
\cdes(w^{-1})=k\end{subarray}}t^{\basc(w)}.\qed

Taking
k=2n-1
in
Equation

25
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 15
.

The
h^{*}
-polynomial of the type C fundamental parallelepiped is

h^{*}_{\Pi_{C_{n}}}(t)=\sum_{w\in X_{n}}t^{\basc(w)}=\Psi_{C_{n}}(t),

where
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)
is the polynomial defined in
Equation

15
.

Example 16
.

We can read from
Figure

2
that

h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{2},1}}(t)=1,\qquad h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{2},2}}(t)=2t,\qquad h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{2},3}}(t)=t;\qquad\text{and}

h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{3},1}}(t)=1,\quad h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{3},2}}(t)=5t+t^{2},\quad h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{3},3}}(t)=5t+5t^{2},\quad h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{3},4}}(t)=3t+3t^{2},\quad h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{3},5}}(t)=t.

Consequently,

h^{*}_{\Pi_{C_{2}}}(t)=1+3t,\qquad\text{and}\qquad h^{*}_{\Pi_{C_{3}}}(t)=1+14t+9t^{2}.

More values of
h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}(t)
and
h^{*}_{\Pi_{C_{n}}}(t)
can be found in
.

5.4.
Type C circular descents (revisited)

In this section, we refine the circular descent statistic of Lam and Postnikov (
Definition

6
) according to our choice of simple roots for the root system of type
C_{n}
.

Definition 17
.

We say that a signed permutation
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
has a
circular descent
at position
i\in[\![n]\!]
if
w_{i}>w_{i^{+}}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\CDes(w)}
denote the set of circular descents of
w
.

Circular descents are easy to read using the complete notation of
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
. Namely, a position
i\in[\![n]\!]
is a circular descent of
w
if, in its complete notation
\widetilde{w}
, the letter
w_{i}
is (cyclically) followed by a smaller letter.

Example 18
.

Consider the signed permutation
u\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
with complete notation

\widetilde{u}=\overline{5}\,\overline{1}\,\underset{\!{}^{\;\circ}}{4}\,\underset{\!{}^{\;\circ}}{2}\,\overline{3}\,\underset{\!{}^{\;\circ}}{3}\,\underset{\!{}^{\;\circ}}{\overline{2}}\,\overline{4}\,1\,\underset{\!{}^{\;\circ}}{5}.

The marked positions show its circular descents.
That is,
\CDes(u)=\{\overline{3},\overline{2},1,2,5\}
and
\cdes(u)=5
.

The next result follows by a direct comparison between the definition of the statistic
\cdes
and of the set
\CDes
.
For completeness, we include its proof.

Lemma 19
.

For all
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
,

\cdes(w)=|\CDes(w)|.

Proof.

Recall that

\cdes(w)=d_{0}(w)+d_{1}(w)+2d_{2}(w)+\dots+2d_{n}(w),

where

•

d_{0}(w)=1\Leftrightarrow w(-2e_{n})\in\Phi^{-}\Leftrightarrow w_{n}>0\Leftrightarrow w_{n}>w_{\overline{n}}\Leftrightarrow n\in\CDes(w)
;

•

d_{1}(w)=1\Leftrightarrow w(2e_{1})\in\Phi^{-}\Leftrightarrow w_{1}<0\Leftrightarrow w_{1}<w_{\overline{1}}\Leftrightarrow\overline{1}\in\CDes(w)
;

•

for
2\leq i\leq n
,

d_{i}(w)=1\Leftrightarrow w(e_{i}-e_{i-1})\in\Phi^{-}\Leftrightarrow w_{i-1}>w_{i}\Leftrightarrow i-1\in\CDes(w)
.

Since for
i\in[n-1]
,
i\in\CDes(w)
if and only if
\overline{i+1}\in\CDes(w)
, this proves the claimed equality.
∎

Remark 20
.

The statistic
\cdes
depends on the choice of simple system for
\Phi
.
We warn the reader that for the other usual choice of simple roots for the type C root system, the description of
\cdes
in
[
26
, Section 12]
contains a minor typo.
Indeed, for
i\in[n-1]
,
w
has a descent (in the Lam-Postnikov convention) if either
w_{i}>w_{i+1}
and both have the same sign, or if
w_{i}<0<w_{i+1}
.

In what follows, it will be important to have an explicit description of the circular descents of the inverse of a signed permutation
w
. The complete notation of
w
will again play an important role.

Lemma 21
.

For
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
and
i\in[\![n]\!]
,

i\in\CDes(w^{-1})\quad\text{if and only if}\quad i^{+}\text{ precedes }i\text{ in }\widetilde{w}.

Observe that
\overline{1}
is a circular descent of
w^{-1}
if and only if
w^{-1}(1)<0
.
Therefore,
X_{n}
is exactly the set of signed permutations
w
for which
\overline{1}\notin\CDes(w^{-1})
.

Example 22
.

Consider the signed permutation
w=4\,\overline{2}\,1\,\overline{3}\,5\in X_{5}
. It is the inverse of the permutation in
Example

18
, so
\CDes(w^{-1})=\{\overline{3},\overline{2},1,2,5\}
. The complete notation of
w
is

\widetilde{w}=\overline{5}\,3\,\overline{1}\,2\,\overline{4}\,4\,\overline{2}\,1\,\overline{3}\,5.

We see, for instance, that
2\in\CDes(w^{-1})
because
3=2^{+}
precedes
2
in
\widetilde{w}
.
Similarly,
\overline{2}\in\CDes(w^{-1})
since
\overline{1}=\overline{2}^{+}
appears before
\overline{2}
;
and
5\in\CDes(w^{-1})
since
\overline{5}=5^{+}
appears before
5
in
\widetilde{w}
.

5.5.
Poset isomorphism

In this section we construct an isomorphism
A:X_{n}\to\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
by explicitly constructing the vertices of the alcove
A(w)
for each signed permutation
w\in X_{n}
; thereby proving
Theorem

14
.

Given
w\in X_{n}
, let
v^{n+1}(w)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the point with coordinates

(26)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:def-trans-vector}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}v^{n+1}_{i}(w)}:=\left|[i-1]\cap\CDes(w^{-1})\right|,\quad\text{ for }i=1,2,\dots,n.

Thus,
v^{n+1}_{1}(w)=0
. For
k\in[n]
, let
v^{k}(w)\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be the point

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}v^{k}(w)}:=v^{k+1}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{w_{k}}=v^{n+1}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}\big(e_{w_{k}}+\dots+e_{w_{n}}\big).

Example 23
.

Let
w=4\,\overline{2}\,1\,\overline{3}\,5\in X_{5}
be the signed permutation from
Example

22
.
Then,

[n-1]\cap\CDes(w^{-1})=[4]\cap\CDes(w^{-1})=\{1,2\},

and

\displaystyle v^{6}(w)

\displaystyle=(0,1,2,2,2);

\displaystyle v^{5}(w)=v^{6}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{5}

\displaystyle=(0,1,2,2,\tfrac{5}{2});

\displaystyle v^{4}(w)=v^{5}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{\overline{3}}

\displaystyle=(0,1,\tfrac{3}{2},2,\tfrac{5}{2});

\displaystyle v^{3}(w)=v^{4}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{1}

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{1}{2},1,\tfrac{3}{2},2,\tfrac{5}{2});

\displaystyle v^{2}(w)=v^{3}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{\overline{2}}

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2},2,\tfrac{5}{2});

\displaystyle v^{1}(w)=v^{2}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{4}

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2},\tfrac{5}{2},\tfrac{5}{2}).

Since
\{e_{w_{1}},\dots,e_{w_{n}}\}
is a linear basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the vectors
v^{1}(w),\dots,v^{n+1}(w)
are affinely independent and their Convex hull is a full-dimensional simplex.

Definition 24
.

Given
w\in X_{n}
, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A(w)}
be the simplex

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}A(w)}:=\Conv\{v^{k}(w)\mid k=1,\dots,n+1\}.

Example 25
.

For the identity permutation
{\rm id}_{n}:=12\cdots n
,
\CDes({\rm id}_{n}^{-1})=\{n\}
thus
v^{n+1}({\rm id}_{n})
is the zero vector and
v^{k}({\rm id}_{n})=(0,\cdots,0,\tfrac{1}{2},\cdots,\tfrac{1}{2})
has
n-k+1
nonzero coordinates. This is exactly the fundamental alcove; that is,
A({\rm id}_{n})=A_{\circ}
.

Proof of
Theorem

14
.

We prove that the map
w\to A(w)
defines a poset isomorphism
A:X_{n}\to\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
.
To do so, we first show that
A
is a well-defined bijection, and then we show that it is both order-preserving and order-reflecting.

The group
\widetilde{W}_{C_{n}}
contains all the translations by integer vectors.
The simplex
A(w)
is the translation of the alcove
w(A_{\circ})
by the integer vector
v^{n+1}(w)
in
Equation

26
, and is therefore an alcove of
\mathcal{H}_{C_{n}}
.
To show that
A(w)\in\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
, in fact that
A(w)
is contained in the hypersimplex
\Delta_{C_{n},\cdes(w^{-1})}
, we verify that the vertices of
A(w)
satisfy the inequalities in
Equation

4
.
Fix
w\in X_{n}
and write
v^{j}=v^{j}(w)
for all
j\in[n+1]
.

•

2v_{1}^{n+1}=2|\varnothing|=0
and, since
1
appears in the window notation of
w
,

v_{1}^{j}\in\{v_{1}^{n+1},v_{1}^{n+1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\}
for all
j
.
Therefore,

0\leq 2v_{1}^{j}\leq 1\text{ for all }j.

•

For
k=2,\dots,n
, we have

v^{n+1}_{k}-v^{n+1}_{k-1}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }k-1\in\CDes(w^{-1}),\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
k-1\in\CDes(w^{-1})
, then by
Lemma

21
we have that

k
precedes
k-1
and
\overline{k-1}
precedes
\overline{k}
in
\widetilde{w}
.
Thus, if
k
(resp.
\overline{k-1}
) appears in the window notation of
w
, and therefore some
v^{j}
is obtained from
v^{j+1}
by adding
\tfrac{1}{2}e_{k}
(resp.
-\tfrac{1}{2}e_{k-1}
), then
\tfrac{1}{2}e_{k-1}
(resp.
-\tfrac{1}{2}e_{k}
) was added for an earlier vertex
v^{j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}>j
. Hence,
\tfrac{1}{2}\leq v^{j}_{k}-v^{j}_{k-1}\leq 1
for all
j
.
A similar analysis shows that if
k-1\notin\CDes(w^{-1})
, then
0\leq v^{j}_{k}-v^{j}_{k-1}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
for all
j
.
In any case, we have

0\leq v^{j}_{k}-v^{j}_{k-1}\leq 1\text{ for all }j.

•

2v^{n+1}_{n}=2|[n-1]\cap\CDes(w)|=\begin{cases}\cdes(w^{-1})-1&\text{if }n\in\CDes(w^{-1}),\\
\cdes(w^{-1})&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
n\in\CDes(w^{-1})
, then
n
appears in the window notation of
w
and therefore

v_{n}^{j}\in\{v_{n}^{n+1},v_{n}^{n+1}+\tfrac{1}{2}\}
for all
j
.
If, on the other hand,
n\notin\CDes(w^{-1})
, then
\overline{n}
appears in the window notation of
w
and therefore

v_{n}^{j}\in\{v_{n}^{n+1},v_{n}^{n+1}-\tfrac{1}{2}\}
for all
j
.
In both cases, we have

\cdes(w^{-1})-1\leq 2v_{n}^{j}\leq\cdes(w^{-1})\text{ for all }j.

Thus
A(w)\subseteq\Delta_{C_{n},\cdes(w^{-1})}\subseteq\Pi_{C_{n}}
.
Observe that
w
can be recovered from
A(w)
by first ordering its vertices according to their number of non-integer coordinates and then keeping track of what coordinate becomes increases/decreases from one vertex to the next.
Therefore, since both
X_{n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{C_{n}}
have the same cardinality (see
Equation

13
), the map
A
is a bijection.

We will now prove that
A(u)\lessdot A(w)
if and only if
u\rightharpoonup w
.

First, suppose
u,w\in X_{n}
are such that
A(u)\lessdot A(w)
.
Let
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
such that the common facet of
A(u)
and
A(w)
lies on a hyperplane of the form
H_{\alpha,p}
.
Given that the hyperplane
H_{\alpha,p}
cuts the interior of
\Pi_{C_{n}}
, the root
\alpha
is not simple.
Moreover, since the reflection of a lattice point across
H_{\alpha,p}
does not change the number of non-integer coordinates, there is a
k\in[n+1]
such that
v^{j}(u)=v^{j}(w)
for
j\neq k
and
v^{k}(w)=v^{k}(u)+\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha
.

Case 1:

If
k=1
,
then
u_{i}=w_{i}
for all
i>1
and necessarily
u_{1}=\overline{w_{1}}
.
Moreover, since

v^{1}(w)-v^{1}(u)=(v^{2}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{w_{1}})-(v^{2}(u)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{u_{1}})=e_{w_{1}}

is, up to scaling, a positive but not simple root,
w_{1}\geq 2
.
Thus,
u\rightharpoonup w
by the first case in
Definition

12
.

Case 2:

If
k\in[2,n]
,
then
u_{i}=w_{i}
for all
i\neq k-1,k
and since

\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{w_{k-1}}+e_{w_{k}})=v^{k-1}(w)-v^{k+1}(w)=v^{k-1}(u)-v^{k+1}(u)=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{u_{k-1}}+e_{u_{k}}),

necessarily
u_{k-1}=w_{k}
and
u_{k}=w_{k-1}
.
Moreover, since

v^{k}(w)-v^{k}(u)=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{w_{k}}-e_{u_{k}})=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{w_{k}}-e_{w_{k-1}})

is, up to scaling, a positive but not simple root,
w_{k-1}\ll w_{k}
.
Thus,
u\rightharpoonup w
by the second case in
Definition

12
.

Case 3:

If
k=n+1
,
then
u_{i}=w_{i}
for all
i<n
and necessarily
u_{n}=\overline{w_{n}}
.
Moreover, since

v^{n+1}(w)-v^{n+1}(u)=(v^{n}(w)-\tfrac{1}{2}e_{w_{n}})-(v^{n}(u)-\tfrac{1}{2}e_{u_{n}})=-e_{w_{n}}

is, up to scaling, a positive but not simple root,
w_{n}\leq\overline{2}
.
Thus,
u\rightharpoonup w
by the third case in
Definition

12
.

Now we prove the reverse implication. Suppose
u,w\in X_{n}
are such that
u\rightharpoonup w
.
We will show that
A(u)
and
A(w)
share
n
vertices and that, for the non-common vertices,
v^{k}(w)-v^{k}(u)
is in the direction of a positive root; thereby showing that
A(u)\lessdot A(w)
.

Case 1:

If
u=\overline{w_{1}}w_{2}\dots w_{n}
and
w_{1}\in[2,n]
, then

\CDes(w^{-1})=\begin{cases}\CDes(w^{-1})\cup\{n\}&\text{if }\overline{u_{1}}=n,\\
\CDes(w^{-1})&\text{if }\overline{u_{1}}\in[2,n-1].\end{cases}

In any case,
\CDes(w^{-1})\cap[n-1]=\CDes(u^{-1})\cap[n-1]
and
v^{j}(w)=v^{j}(u)
for
2\leq j\leq n+1
.
For
j=1
, since
w_{2}\geq 2
,

v^{1}(w)-w^{1}(u)=(v^{2}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{w_{1}})-(v^{2}(u)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{\overline{w_{1}}})=e_{w_{1}}\in\tfrac{1}{2}\Phi^{+}_{C_{n}}.

Case 2:

If
u=w_{1}\dots w_{i+1}w_{i}\dots w_{n}
for some
i\in[n-1]
and
w_{i}\ll w_{i+1}
, then

\CDes(w^{-1})=\CDes(u^{-1}),

since
w_{i}
and
w_{i+1}
(and
\overline{w_{i}}
and
\overline{w_{i+1}}
) are not cyclically adjacent and they are the only entries that change positions between
\widetilde{u}
and
\widetilde{w}
.
Thus,
v^{j}(w)=v^{j}(u)
for
j\neq i+1
.
For
j=i+1
, since
w_{i}\ll w_{i+1}
,

v^{i+1}(w)-v^{i+1}(u)=(v^{i+2}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{w_{i+1}})-(v^{i+2}(u)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{w_{i}})=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{w_{i+1}}-e_{w_{i}})\in\tfrac{1}{2}\Phi^{+}_{C_{n}}.

Case 3:

If
w_{n}=\overline{r}
for some
r\in[2,n]
and
u=w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n-1}r
, then

\CDes(w^{-1})=\begin{cases}\CDes(u^{-1})\setminus\{n\}\sqcup\{n-1,\overline{n}\}&\text{if }r=n,\\
\CDes(u^{-1})\setminus\{r,\overline{r+1}\}\sqcup\{r-1,\overline{r}\}&\text{if }r\leq n-1.\end{cases}

In both cases,
|\CDes(w^{-1})\cap[j-1]|=|\CDes(u^{-1})\cap[j-1]|+\delta_{j,r}
. Hence,

v^{n+1}(w)-v^{n+1}(u)=e_{r}\in\tfrac{1}{2}\Phi^{+}_{C_{n}}.

Moreover, since

v^{n}(w)=v^{n+1}(w)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{\overline{r}}=(v^{n+1}(u)+e_{r})+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{\overline{r}}=v^{n+1}(u)+\tfrac{1}{2}e_{r}=v^{n}(u),

it follows that
v^{j}(w)=v^{j}(u)
for all
j\leq n
.
∎

6.
On the
h^{*}
-polynomial of the closed hypersimplices

In this section, we relate the
h^{*}
-polynomials of the closed
\Delta_{C_{n},k}
and the half-open
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}
hypersimplices.

Proposition 26
.

For all
n>0
and
1\leq k\leq 2n-1
,

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\Delta_{C_{n},k}}(t)=

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}(t)+(1-t)\left(h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n-1},k-1}}(t)+h^{*}_{\Delta_{C_{n-1},k-2}}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}}(t)+\sum_{j\geq 1}(1-t)^{j}\left(h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n-j},k-2j+1}}(t)+h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n-j},k-2j}}(t)\right).

Proof.

We only prove the first equality since the second follows from it by induction.

Recall from
Equation

18
that

\Delta_{C_{n},k}=\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}\sqcup\left(\Delta_{C_{n},k}\cap H_{2e_{n},k-1}\right).

The intersection
\Delta_{C_{n},k}\cap H_{2e_{n},k-1}=\Pi_{C_{n}}\cap H_{2e_{n},k-1}
is determined by the following (in)equalities

0\leq 2x_{1},x_{2}-x_{1},\dots,x_{n}-x_{n-1}\leq 1\qquad\text{and}\qquad 2x_{n}=k-1.

Observe that
0\leq\tfrac{k-1}{2}-x_{n-1}\leq 1
if and only if
k-3\leq 2x_{n-1}\leq k-1
.
Moreover, the linear isomorphism
H_{2e_{n},k-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
given by the projection that forgets the last coordinate
induces an isomorphism on the lattices
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\cap H_{2e_{n},k-1}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
.
Therefore,
\Delta_{C_{n},k}\cap H_{2e_{n},k-1}
is integrally equivalent to

\Delta_{C_{n-1},k-2}\cup\Delta_{C_{n-1},k-1}=\Delta_{C_{n-1},k-2}\sqcup\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n-1},k-1}
.
The result follows since these polytopes have dimension one less that
\Delta_{C_{n},k}
.
∎

Example 27
.

We apply the formula above for
n=3
and
k=2,k=4
.

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\Delta_{C_{3},2}}(t)=

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\Delta_{C^{\prime}_{3},2}}(t)+(1-t)h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{2},1}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(5t+t^{2})+(1-t)=1+4t+t^{2}.

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\Delta_{C_{3},4}}(t)=

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\Delta_{C^{\prime}_{3},4}}(t)+(1-t)\left(h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{2},3}}(t)+h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{2},2}}(t)\right)+(1-t)^{2}h^{*}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{1},1}}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(3t+3t^{2})+(1-t)(t+2t)+(1-t)^{2}=1+4t+t^{2}.

More values of
h^{*}_{\Delta_{C_{n},k}}(t)
can be found in
.

7.
On the
h^{*}
-polynomial of the fundamental parallelepiped

We now turn our attention to the
h^{*}
-polynomial of the fundamental parallelepiped
\Pi_{C_{n}}
, which, by
Corollary

15
, corresponds to the polynomial
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)
defined in
Equation

15
. For
n\geq 1
and
0\leq k\leq n
, we define

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\psi_{n,k}}:=|\{w\in X_{n}\mid\basc(w)=k\}|,

so that
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)=\sum_{k}\psi_{n,k}t^{k}
. We will show that the numbers
\psi_{n,k}
exhibit behavior analogous to the Eulerian numbers. To begin, we present an explicit recursion for
\psi_{n,k}
, which can be proven combinatorially.

Proposition 28
.

The numbers
\psi_{n,k}
are completely determined by the following recurrence.
For all
n>1
,

(27)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:recurrence}\psi_{n,k}=(2n-2k+1)\psi_{n-1,k-1}+(2k+1)\psi_{n-1,k},

with initial conditions
\psi_{1,0}=1
and
\psi_{1,k}=0
for
k\neq 0
.

Proof.

Given
w\in X_{n}
, consider the signed permutation (in
X_{n-1}
) obtained by removing
\pm n
from the window notation of
w
.
Then, the statistic
\basc
is either unchanged or it decreases by exactly
1
.
Indeed, to increase the statistic
\basc
by removing a letter
w_{i}
, we need
w_{i-1}\not\!\!\ll w_{i}\not\!\!\ll w_{i+1}
and
w_{i-1}\ll w_{i+1}
.
But this can only occur if
w_{i-1},w_{i},w_{i+1}
are consecutive in the usual order of
[\![n]\!]
.
Since we are removing either the minimum (
\overline{n}
) or maximum (
n
) element of
[\![n]\!]
,
\basc
cannot increase.

To conclude the recurrence in (
27
), we show that if
w\in X_{n-1}
has
\basc(w)=k
,
then exactly
2k+1
out of the
2n
possible insertions of
\pm n
do not increase the statistic
\basc
.

Let
w\in X_{n-1}
with
\basc(w)=k
.
Take a big ascent position
i\in\BAsc(w)
, including the edge cases
i=\overline{1}
(
w_{1}\leq\overline{2}
) and
i=n-1
(
w_{n-1}\geq 2
).
Note that
w_{i}\ll w_{i^{+}}
implies both
w_{i}\neq n-1
and
w_{i^{+}}\neq\overline{n-1}
,
the maximum and minimum elements, respectively.
Then, inserting
n
(
w_{i}\ll n>w_{i+1}
)
or
\overline{n}
(
w_{i}>\overline{n}\ll w_{i+1}
) after
w_{i}
,
does not change the statistic
\basc
.
In addition, if
n-1
(resp.
\overline{n-1}
) appears in the window notation of in
w
,
we can insert
n
after
n-1
(resp.
\overline{n}
before
\overline{n-1}
) without changing
\basc
.
Inserting
\pm n
in any other position will increase
\basc
by one
(either
w_{j}<n\gg w_{j+1}
or
w_{j}\gg\overline{n}<w_{j+1}
).
∎

Figure 3
.
The first values of
\psi_{n,k}
computed using the recurrence in
Equation

27
.
For instance,
\psi_{5,2}=(2\cdot 5-2\cdot 2+1)\cdot 49+(2\cdot 2+1)\cdot 115=918
.
Each number is the weighted sum of the values directly north-east and north-west of it,
and the weights are constant along the diagonals.
See the last row of
for more values of
\psi_{n,k}
.

Corollary 29

(Linear polynomial recurrence)
.

For any
n\geq 2
,

\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)=(1+(2n-1)t)\Psi_{C_{n-1}}(t)+2t(1-t)\Psi^{\prime}_{C_{n-1}}(t).

Remark 30
.

The type B Eulerian polynomials
E_{B_{n}}(t):=\sum_{k}B_{n,k}t^{k}
also satisfy the linear polynomial recurrence in
Corollary

29
;
see for instance Petersen’s book
[
30
, Theorem 13.2]
.
The two families of polynomials are therefore distinguished by their initial conditions:

\Psi_{C_{1}}(t)=1
while
E_{B_{1}}(t)=1+t
.
We discuss further relations between
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)
and
E_{B_{n}}(t)
in
Section

8
below.

One can use the recurrence in
Corollary

29
to write a differential equation that determines the generating function of the polynomials
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)
and try to deduce a closed formula for it. Instead, we give a more elegant derivation of this generating function using Ehrhart theory.

7.1.
Generating function of big ascents

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rm Eul}_{A}(t,x)}
be the exponential generating function for the classical Eulerian polynomials
E_{A_{n}}(t)
:

(28)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:generatin-Eulerian}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}{\rm Eul}_{A}(t,x)}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}E_{A_{n}}(t)\dfrac{x^{n}}{n!}=\dfrac{t-1}{t-e^{x(t-1)}}.

We refer the reader to Foata’s survey
[
15
, Section 3]
for a derivation of this formula.

Theorem

E
.

The following identity holds

\sum_{n\geq 0}h^{*}_{\Pi_{C_{n+1}}}(t)\frac{x^{n}}{n!}=e^{3x(t-1)}{\rm Eul}_{A}(t,2x)^{2}.

Proof.

Using the same change of coordinates as in the proof of
Proposition

9
, we deduce that
\Pi_{C_{n}}
, with lattice
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, is integrally equivalent to the box
[0,1]\times[0,2]^{n-1}
, with the usual integer lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
Hence, the Ehrhart polynomial of
\Pi_{C_{n}}
is
L_{\Pi_{C_{n}}}(k)=(k+1)(2k+1)^{n-1}
.
Therefore,

(29)

\addcontentsline{lla}{section}{\numberline q:generating-aux}\dfrac{\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)}{(1-t)^{n+1}}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(2k+1)^{n-1}(k+1)t^{k}.

Multiplying both sides of the equation above by
\tfrac{x^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}
and taking the sum over
n\geq 1
, we obtain

\dfrac{1}{(1-t)^{2}}\sum_{n\geq 0}\dfrac{\Psi_{C_{n+1}}(t)}{(1-t)^{n}}\dfrac{x^{n}}{n!}=\sum_{n,k\geq 0}(2k+1)^{n}(k+1)t^{k}\dfrac{x^{n}}{n!}=\\
\sum_{k\geq 0}(k+1)t^{k}e^{((2k+1)x)}=e^{x}\sum_{k\geq 0}(k+1)(te^{2x})^{k}=\dfrac{e^{x}}{(1-te^{2x})^{2}}.

Finally, multiplying both sides by
(t-1)^{2}
and substituting
x:=x(1-t)
we get

\sum_{n\geq 0}\Psi_{C_{n+1}}(t)\dfrac{x^{n}}{n!}=\dfrac{e^{x(1-t)}(t-1)^{2}}{(1-te^{2x(1-t)})^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{e^{4x(t-1)}}{e^{4x(t-1)}}=e^{3x(t-1)}\left(\dfrac{t-1}{t-e^{2x(t-1)}}\right)^{2}.

The result follows by comparing the right hand side with
Equation

28
.
∎

As a consequence of the proof, we show that the polynomials
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)
have real roots for all
n\geq 1
.
We use the following result of Brenti
[
7
, Theorem 2.2]
, which he used to show the real-rootedness of the Eulerian polynomials of type B.

Theorem 31

(Brenti)
.

Let

f(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}b_{i}\binom{t+d-i}{d}

be a polynomial of degree
d
.
Suppose that
f(t)
has all its roots in the interval
[-1,0]
.
Then the polynomial
\sum_{i=0}^{d}b_{i}t^{i}
has only real roots.

Theorem 32
.

For all
n\geq 1
, the polynomial
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)
is real-rooted.

Proof.

Equation

29
shows that

\sum_{k}\psi_{n,k}\binom{t+n-k}{n}=(2t+1)^{n-1}(t+1).

Clearly, all the roots of
(2t+1)^{n-1}(t+1)
, namely
-1
and
-\tfrac{1}{2}
, lie on the interval
[-1,0]
.
Therefore, by Brenti’s theorem above, the polynomial
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)=\sum_{k}\psi_{n,k}t^{k}
is real-rooted.
∎

8.
Relations with type B Eulerian numbers, two ways

In type A, the Eulerian numbers appear both as the
h^{*}
-polynomial of the fundamental parallelepiped (or equivalently, as the cover-counting statistic on the poset of cyclic permutations of
[
1
]
) and as the volumes of the hypersimplices. In this section, we explore analogous results for type C. An analog of the volume formula was not expected, as there are
2n-1
hypersimplices of type
C_{n}
but only
n+1
possible values for an Eulerian statistic on
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
.

8.1.
Big ascents and Coxeter descents

If we use
Theorem

B
instead of
Theorem

A
to compute the
h^{*}
-polynomial of
\Pi_{C_{n}}
from that of the half-open hypersimplices
\Delta^{\prime}_{C_{n},k}
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 33
.

For all
n\geq 1
,

\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)=h^{*}_{\Pi_{C_{n}}}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{w\in X_{n}}t^{\des_{B}(w)}.

Since for all
w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}
we have
\des_{B}(w)+\des_{B}(\overline{w})=n
, and
\mathfrak{B}_{n}=X_{n}\sqcup\overline{X_{n}}
, we deduce the following.

Corollary 34
.

For all
n\geq 1
,

\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)+t^{n}\Psi_{C_{n}}(t^{-1})=E_{B_{n}}(t),

where
E_{B_{n}}(t)
is the type B Eulerian polynomial.

For example, for
n=3
we have

\Psi_{C_{3}}(t)+t^{3}\Psi_{C_{3}}(t^{-1})=(1+14t+9t^{2})+(9t+14t^{2}+t^{3})=1+23t+23t^{2}+t^{3}=E_{B_{3}}(t).

Remark 35
.

Corollary

33
implies that the polynomial
\Psi_{C_{n}}(t)
agrees with the polynomial defined by Matthew Hyatt in
[
21
, Theorem 1.1]
. Using different methods, Hyatt also proves the real-rootedness of these polynomials (c.f.
Theorem

32
).

8.2.
Volumes of type C hypersimplices and type B Eulerian numbers

Let
w_{0}=\overline{n}\dots\overline{2}\,\overline{1}
be the longest element in
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
. Then,
\cdes(w_{0}w)=\cdes(w)
for any signed permutation
w
. Thus, plugging
t=1
in
Theorem

A
recovers the formula for the volume of the type C hypersimplices from Lam-Postnikov;
Equation

17
:

\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},k})=|\{w\in X_{n}\mid\cdes(w)=k\}|=\dfrac{1}{2}|\{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\mid\cdes(w)=k\}|.

Plugging
t=1
in
Theorem

B
, yields a different formula.

Corollary

C
.

For all
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
,

\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},k})=|\{w\in X_{n}\mid\fexc(w)=k-1\}|.

By comparing both formulas, one deduces that
\fexc+1
and
\cdes
have the same distribution on
X_{n}
. However, their distribution over all
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
differ; as
\fexc
takes
2n
different values while
\cdes
only takes
2n-1
values.

Corollary

D
.

For all
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
, we have:

B_{n,k}=\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k-1})+2\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k})+\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k+1}),

where
\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},j})=0
whenever
j<1
or
j>2n-1
.

Proof.

Recall from
Section

2.1
that the statistic
\exc_{B}(w):=\left\lfloor\tfrac{\fexc(w)+1}{2}\right\rfloor
is Eulerian. Moreover, a signed permutation
w\in X_{n}
satisfies
\fexc(w)=r
if and only if the signed permutation
w^{\prime}\in\overline{X_{n}}
obtained from
w
by negating
1
satisfies
\fexc(w^{\prime})=r+1
. Thus,

\displaystyle B_{n,k}

\displaystyle=|\{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\mid\exc_{B}(w)=k\}|

\displaystyle=|\{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\mid\fexc(w)=2k-1\}|+|\{w\in\mathfrak{B}_{n}\mid\fexc(w)=2k\}|

\displaystyle=|\{w\in X_{n}\mid\fexc(w)=2k-2\}|+2|\{w\in X_{n}\mid\fexc(w)=2k-1\}|+|\{w\in X_{n}\mid\fexc(w)=2k\}|

\displaystyle=\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k-1})+2\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k})+\Vol(\Delta_{C_{n},2k+1}).

The last step uses
Corollary

C
.
∎

9.
Limit Poset: Strict Partitions

Theorem

E
provides a simple description of the exponential generating function for the distribution of cover relations in
\mathcal{I}_{\Phi_{C_{n}}}\cong(X_{n},\leq)
. This prompts natural questions about the asymptotic behavior of these partial orders. In type A, Laget-Chapelier, Reutenauer, and the first author
[
1
]
proved that
\mathcal{I}_{\Phi_{A_{n}}}
, the weak order on the alcoves contained in
\Pi_{A_{n}}
, converges to Young’s lattice of partitions as
n
goes to infinity. We establish an analogous result for
(X_{n},\leq)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{P}}
denote
Young’s lattice
, which is the partial order on integer partitions given by the containment of their corresponding Ferrers diagrams. The collection of
strict partitions
–partitions in which all parts are distinct–forms the sublattice
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{SP}}
.
Figure

4
(right) shows the Hasse diagram of
\mathcal{SP}
truncated at rank
6
.

12
1\overline{2}
\overline{2}1
123
12\overline{3}
1\overline{3}2
1\overline{3}\overline{2}
\overline{3}12
\overline{3}1\overline{2}
\overline{3}\overline{2}1
\varnothing

Figure 4
.

Subposets
(Y_{n},\leq)
for
n=2,3
(left),
and their embedding in the lattice of strict partitions (right),
represented using
shifted diagrams
(in French notation).

Consider the order ideal
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}Y_{n}}:=\{w\in X_{n}\mid\cdes(w^{-1})\leq 2\}
of
X_{n}
.
It consists of those signed permutations
w\in X_{n}
whose associated alcoves
A(w)
lie inside
\Delta_{C_{n},1}\cup\Delta_{C_{n},2}
.
Let
\tau_{n}:Y_{n}\to X_{n+1}
be the function
\tau(w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n})=1w^{\prime}_{1}\,w^{\prime}_{2}\cdots w^{\prime}_{n}
where
w_{i}^{\prime}\in[\![n+1]\!]
has the same sign as
w_{i}
and satisfies
|w^{\prime}_{i}|=|w_{i}|+1
.
For example,

\tau_{2}(1\overline{2})=12\overline{3}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{3}(\overline{3}1\overline{2})=1\overline{4}2\overline{3}.

Theorem 36
.

For all
n\geq 1
,
\tau_{n}(Y_{n})\subset Y_{n+1}
and
\tau_{n}:Y_{n}\to Y_{n+1}
is a poset embedding.
Furthermore, the lattice of strict partitions
\mathcal{SP}
is the colimit in the category of posets of the diagram

Y_{1}\xrightarrow{\tau_{1}}Y_{2}\xrightarrow{\tau_{2}}Y_{3}\xrightarrow{\tau_{3}}\dots.

Proof.

The hyperplane separating
\Delta_{C_{n},2}
and
\Delta_{C_{n},3}
is given by the equation
\langle x,2e_{n}\rangle=2
.
Thus, the elements
w\in Y_{n}
are those obtained from the identity
1\mkern 1.0mu2\dots n
by applying the relations in
23
and changing the sign of
n
at most once.
Therefore, the window notation of elements
w\in Y_{n}
are precisely the shuffles of the words
1\mkern 1.0mu2\dots k
and
\overline{n}\dots\overline{k+1}
for some
1\leq k\leq n
.
Define a map
\lambda_{n}:Y_{n}\to\mathcal{SP}
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\lambda_{n}(w)}:=(n+1-i\mid i\in[n]\,,\,w_{i}<0).

For example,

\lambda_{6}(1\mkern 1.0mu2\mkern 1.0mu3\mkern 1.0mu4\mkern 1.0mu5\mkern 1.0mu\overline{6})=(1)={\begin{gathered}\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}}
, and

\lambda_{6}(1\mkern 1.0mu\overline{6}\mkern 1.0mu2\mkern 1.0mu3\mkern 1.0mu\overline{5}\mkern 1.0mu\overline{4})=(5,2,1)={\begin{gathered}\hbox to28.85pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{gathered}}
.
It follows form the form of the cover relations in
Y_{n}
that
\lambda_{n}
sends cover relations in
Y_{n}
to cover relations in
\mathcal{SP}
.

Since
\tau_{n}
only adds a positive letter in the first position,
\lambda_{n+1}\circ\tau_{n}=\lambda_{n}
.
This shows that
\mathcal{SP}
(together with the maps
\lambda_{n}
) is a
cocone
over the diagram
\big(Y_{1}\xrightarrow{\tau_{1}}Y_{2}\xrightarrow{\tau_{2}}\dots\big)
.
To prove that
\mathcal{SP}
is in fact the colimit of this diagram, it suffices to observe that any strict partition
\mu
with
\mu_{1}\leq n
determines a unique shuffle
w
of
1,\dots,k
and
\overline{n}\dots\overline{k+1}
such that
\lambda_{n}(w)=\mu
and
k=n-\ell(\mu)
: the shuffle for which the negative letters appear in positions

n+1-\mu_{1},n+1-\mu_{2},\dots,n+1-\mu_{\ell(\mu)}
.
∎

Observe that the first step of the proof shows that, in particular,
Y_{n}
contains all the elements
w\in X_{n}
of rank at most
n
. Given that the morphisms
Y_{n-1}\to Y_{n}
preserve rank, we deduce the following result.

Corollary 37
.

For every
N
and
n\geq N
, the truncation of
Y_{n}
to elements of rank at most
N
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{SP}_{\leq N}
.

Corollary 38
.

For any
w\in Y_{n}
, the number of maximal chains
{\rm id}_{n}\rightharpoonup\dots\rightharpoonup w
in
X_{n}

is the number of shifted standard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda_{n}(w)
.

Example 39
.

The signed permutation
w=1\mkern 1.0mu2\mkern 1.0mu3\mkern 1.0mu\overline{6}\mkern 1.0mu4\mkern 1.0mu\overline{5}\in Y_{6}
has negative entries in the fourth and sixth positions,
so
\lambda_{6}(w)=(7-4,7-6)=(3,1)
.
Hence, there are exactly two maximal chains in the interval
[{\rm id}_{6},w]
of
X_{6}
(corresponding to the shifted SYT

10.
Types B and D

We now have a solid understanding for the Ehrhart
h^{*}
-polynomials of hypersimplices for types A and C. In this section, we will explore what is fundamentally different for types B and D and conclude with an intriguing conjecture.

We begin with the case of type B. For
n=1,2
, the root systems of types
B_{n}
and
C_{n}
are isomorphic. Thus, we assume
n\geq 3
. Let
\Phi\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the root system of type
B_{n}
, whose set of simple roots given by

\alpha_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1},\hskip 17.07182pt\text{for }1\leq i\leq n-1,\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\alpha_{n}=e_{n}.

Its highest root is

\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+\dots+2\alpha_{n}=e_{1}+e_{2},

and the nonzero vertices of the fundamental alcove are

\omega_{1}=e_{1},\quad\tfrac{\omega_{2}}{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{1}+e_{2}),\quad\dots,\quad\tfrac{\omega_{n-1}}{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{1}+\dots+e_{n-1}),\quad\tfrac{\omega_{n}}{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}(e_{1}+\dots+e_{n}).

The lattice
\mathcal{L}
spanned by the vertices of the fundamental alcove is

\mathcal{L}=\{v\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\mid v_{1}-v_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

Unlike in type C, the lattice spanned by the vertices of the fundamental alcove does not agree with the set of vertices of all the alcoves in
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
. That is to say,
\mathcal{L}
is not invariant under the action of
\widetilde{W}_{\Phi}
. For instance, the reflection of the point
\tfrac{\omega_{2}}{2}=(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},0)\in\mathcal{L}
across the hyperplane
H_{e_{2}-e_{3},0}\in\mathcal{H}
is the point
(\frac{1}{2},0,\frac{1}{2})\notin\mathcal{L}
. Therefore, not all alcoves are lattice polytopes with respect to
\mathcal{L}
. Alternatively, if we consider the lattice spanned by the vertices of all alcoves, namely
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we find that the alcoves are not unimodular; in fact, they have a normalized volume of
2
.

The same is true in type
D_{n}
, for
n\geq 4
. Namely, the lattice spanned by the vertices of the fundamental alcove is not invariant under the action of
\widetilde{W}_{\Phi}
. In particular, we cannot apply the ideas in
Section

5.3
to compute the
h^{*}
-polynomials of type B and D hypersimplices by studying the weak order restricted to the alcoves contained in the fundamental parallelepiped. However, computational evidence suggests an intriguing relationship between
\Psi_{B_{n}}(t)
and the type D Eulerian polynomial
E_{D_{n}}(t)
.

Conjecture 40
.

For
n\geq 3
,

{\Psi_{B_{n}}(t)+t^{n}\Psi_{B_{n}}(t^{-1})}=2E_{D_{n}}(t).

For example, for
n=4
,

{(1+56t+102t^{2}+32t^{3}+t^{4})+(1+32t+102t^{2}+56t^{3}+t^{4})}=2(1+44t+102t^{2}+44t^{3}+t^{4}).

Compare the conjecture with
Corollary

34
.

1
below show the coefficients of the polynomials
\Psi_{B_{n}}(t)
and
\Psi_{D_{n}}(t)
for small values of
n
.
